Usage guidelines 

Google is proud lo partner with libraries lo digili/e public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order lo keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial panics, including placing Icchnical restrictions on automated querying. 
We also ask that you: 

+ Make n on -commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request thai you use these files for 
personal, non -commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain from automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort lo Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attribution The Google "watermark" you see on each lile is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use. remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 

countries. Whether a book is slill in copyright varies from country lo country, and we can'l offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner

PREFACE. 



The present volume contains abstracts of the remaining protocols in 
Henry Gibsone's second book, and the greater part of the protocols in the 
first book of his successor, Archibald Hegate. Gibsone held the town 
clerkship till 1581, and again in (perhaps before) 1596, and for a few years 
subsequently, but his books between 19th August, 1576, and 29th June, 
1598, are not now extant, with the exception of a single leaf containing 
protocols bearing date 1584-5. 

Archibald Hegate, who succeeded Henry Gibsone as town clerk, was 
a son of William Hegate, burgess of Glasgow, and was admitted a notary 
by the lords of council and session in May or June, 1581 (Appx. No. L). 
At that time Esme, earl of Lennox, cousin of the king, and great 
chamberlain of Scotland, was provost of Glasgow, and he, "with an thorite 
and command of the Kingis majestic," appointed Hegate town clerk. 1 This 
innovation on the town's privileges was acquiesced in by an act entered 
in the burgh court book on 23rd May, 1581; but on 9th September, 1589, 
the magistrates and council ordered the act to be "deleit and put furth of 
the buik, as repugnant and maist prejudiciall to the libertie of the toun, 
they haifand electioun in their awin handis of the said office in all tyme 
bygane." J Previous to that date, however, Hegate had ceased to be town 
clerk. He was re-elected by the magistrates and council in May, 1583, 
and June, 1584. No election is noted in 1585, and the records are wanting 
between April, 1586, and October, 1588. On 20th May, 1589, "maister 
Johnne Ros" was elected clerk. In 1605 and subsequent years Archibald 
Hegate again held the office of town clerk, and his protocol books extend 
in a continuous series from 1581 till 1612. 



l Glasgow Records, L pp. 81, 86. 2 /&., p. 146. 



viii PREFACE. 

After a notary ceased to hold the town clerkship, his transactions 
related chiefly to properties beyond the burgh boundaries; and, accordingly, 
the main loss to local history occasioned by the disappearance of Henry 
Gibsone's protocols applies to the period between 1576 and 1581. This is 
the first large gap in the protocol books subsequent to the Reformation, 
and it occurs at a time when interesting particulars regarding the disposal 
of church property and ecclesiastical endowments might have been looked 
for. No doubt it occasionally happens that a later protocol makes up for 
one that has disappeared, as in No. 2462, which sufficiently explains what 
became of the ancient church of St. Tenu; but in many cases the loss 
of information is complete. 

During the early years of Hegate's clerkship there was considerable 
trouble in municipal and ecclesiastical circles in connection with the appointment 
of Robert Montgomrie, minister at Stirling, as successor to James Boyd, 
archbishop of Glasgow; 1 and, for the part he took in the affair, the clerk 
incurred the displeasure of the presbyterian party. On 9th October, 1582, 
the general assembly directed "that Coline Campbell, burges of Glasgow, 
William Hegget, Archibald Hegget, and their complices, be punished according 
to justice, for the seditioun and uproar made by them, being magistrates 
and counsellors, 2 against the students of Glasgow, and shedding their blood;" 3 
and at the same time it was ordained that certain accomplices of the 
provost, including "Archibald Hogget," should be excommunicated by the 
minister of Glasgow "for hanting the companie of Mr. R. Montgomrie, 
after his excommunication, and other enormiteis committed by them." 4 

As stated in Archibald Hegate's act of admission, the protocol book 
then supplied to him consisted of one hundred and ninety-three leaves, 
authenticated by Robert Scott, clerk to the lords of session. This book 
was regularly written so far as folio 180, where the notary has 
inserted a statement to the effect that some imprudent person had cut out 



8ee Glasgow Charters, I., pp. exx-exxvi. 3 Calderwood's Historie of the Kirk of Scotland 

> No member of council at this time bore the name of (Wodrow Society), ill, pp. 086-7. 

" Archibald Hegyet," and it is supposed that the town <lb., p. 688. 
clerk was meant. 



PREFACE ix 

the next leaf, and that therefore, to avoid suspicion of fraud, he had inserted 
no further protocols in the book. On four of the remaining leaves have been 
written the " authorities and notabill placis of Scripture" reproduced in 
Appendix II. 

The extended topographical knowledge gained by the publication of the 
protocols renders practicable the compilation of a map of old Glasgow in 
fuller detail than has hitherto been attempted; and it is intended that 
one of the three volumes now required to complete the series of Glasgow 
Protocols will contain a detailed map of at least the section of the city 
embracing the cathedral and adjacent streets. 

It may here be explained that though the headline of the printed 
Protocols still retains the years of indiction and pontificate, the protocols 
themselves, at the date now reached, specify neither of these particulars. 
Shortly after the Reformation the practice became irregular, and gradually 
ceased altogether; but there was no fixed rule on the subject, each notary 
apparently following his own inclination. There being thus no definite 
termination of the custom, it was thought as well to keep up both indiction 
and pontificate as items of chronological information. 

R. R. 

Glasgow, June, 1899. 



APPENDIX TO PREFACE. 



L— Act of Admission by the Lords of Council and Session of Archibald 

Hegate as a Notary Public. 

Apud Edinburgh, vigesimo quarto die mensis . . . Anno Domini 
millesimo quingentesimo octingentesimo [primo 1 ]. 

Thee quhilk day, in presence of thee lordis of counsale undirwrittin, viz. : — 
Mr. Johnne Maitland of Thirlestane and Williame Dowglas of Quhittinghame, 
comperit personallie Archibald Hegate, sone lauchtfull to William Hegate, 
burges of Glasgu, being of thee age of xxv yeiris, and producit thee letter 
undirwrittin, subscrivit bee thee Kingis Majestie and his counsale, of thee 
quhilk thee tenour followis: — Rex: Chancelare, presedent, and remanent lordis 
of our counsale and sessioun: Wee greit yow weill. Forsamekle as wee ar 
informit that thee beirrar heirof, Archibald Hegate, is sufficientlie qualifeit 
for using of thee office of notario, bee daylie practizee thairof with notaris 
and utheris directouris of our chancellarie, gif hee war admittit be yow 
thairto. Thairfor it is our will, and wee require yow that yce try and 
examinat his qualificatioun, and finding him worthie for using of thee said 
office that yee admit him thairto, resaif his aith and cautioun for dew 
administratioun thairof, and authorize him with your testimoniall thairupoune 
in dew forme as efferis. Subscrivit with our hand, at Dalkeith, thee vij 
day of Maij, and of our regnnc thee xiiij year, 1581. Sic subscribiiur : 
James R.; Montrois; Alexr. Ersking. Conforme to thee quhilk letter, thee 
said Archibald, being of thee age foirsaid and unmareit, requirit to bee 
admittit in ane publict notar and autentik, to thee using of thee office of 
the samyn in all tymes cuming; and cftir dew examinatioun had be the 
saidis lordis of the said Archibald, undirstanding him to be qualifeit for 
using of the said office in tyme cuming, hes thairfor admittit and admittis 
him thairto; quha hes fund Johnne Boswell of Auchlek cautioun and souirtie 
for him; and the said Archibald oblist him to relleif the said Johnne his 
cautioner foirsaid of the said souirtie and dew administratioun of the said 
office, and refounding of all dampnage, skaith, intres, as the said act maid 
this present day proportis. And hes gevin his aith that he sail leillie and 
trewlie minister the said office, and sail observe and keip the act of 
parliament maid anent notaris in all poyntis. And in signne and taikin of 
his admissioun hes causit ingros his signne and subscriptioun manuell, quhilk

Deuteronomium, Ca. 

Mandatum hoc quod ego precipio tibi hodie non supra te est, [etc. 
Deuteronomy xxx. 11-16]. 

Heir in this place thir lait enemeis of Godis kirk ar confoundit in 
thair fals doctrene quhilk they teich to the pepill, crying and creiking in 
pulpitis that the commandimentis of God may nocht be kepit and obeyit, 
seing that heir God him self sayis to his pepill that his commandymentis 
gifin thame ar nocht abufc thair power, as may lykeuise be sene in the 
young man that come to Chryste and askit him quhat he sould do to get 
evirlasting lyfe, to quhome Chryste ansuerit: Keip the commandymentis. 
The young man ansuerand Chryste, said to him, efter that Jesus had 
repetit the commandimentis, that he had kepit thame fra his youth up; 
and Chryste accusit him nocht of the contrar, bot sayis he lakit ane 
thing, that is, sell that thow hes and gif to the pure, and follow me. 
Quhilk quhen the young man hard, he was sad, etc. Sa we may persaif 
that the yok and burding of Chryste is licht and sweit and nocht ane 
thing impossibill. Yet I will nocht deny bot the commandymentis may be 
brokin, and daylie brokin, bot for the same trew repentance pleissis the 
Lord in the Lamb slayne for the synnes of the wardill. Bot this doctrene 
of Luther and Calvin and thair followeris is to lerne men to flattir thame 
selfis quhen they haif synnit, for gif it be impossibill to keip the 
commandymentis, than ar we at rest with our selfis, becaus God hes 
commandit ane impossibilitie quhilk is aganes his justice and rychteusnes, 
seing Paul says that God is faithfull and sail nocht tempt or lay to our 
charge that quhilk we may nocht.

Libro predicto, Ca. xiij. 

The excuse of Saul to Samuell, hairing made sacrifice to God. 

Quia vidi quod populus dilaberetur, a me, [etc. 1 Samuel, xiii. 11-14]. 

Heir wee are teichit that na manir of persoune, King nor subject 
. . .* usurp or tak on him the spirituall office of the pastour, how good 
that evir thair ... bee without they bee lauchfullie admittit and 
ordinarlie or utherwayis extraordinarilie callit bo God him self, uthirwayis 
all their service or ministratioun of the ... is meir abominatioun in 
the sicht of God. 

Than would I speir the questioun gif this cair of admitting spirituall 
pastouris in the kirk be nocht als necessar now to be observit in thir lettir 
day is in the Christian kirk as in the Jewis kirk, seeing wee ar foirwarnit 
of fals propheitis to cum in the lettir dayis, be Chryste him self, quha sail 
evin pretend the verie trewth and word of God. I think na man will deny 
bot it is als necessar to be observit, ye I may say ane thing commandit 
be Paull, that wee sould prufe and try all spreitis, and nocht to gif haistie 
credit to evirrie wurd of doctrene, and ane thing practizit be the Apostlis, 
as we read in the Actis, sa that nane was admittit to the ministrie of the 
Evangoll bot be thair knawledge and authoritie. Than I demand of Luther, 
Calvin, and all thair ofspring, quhat warrand haif they of thair calling. 
I think thair ofspring man depend of thame, but Luther as the foirfather 
of all can allege na uther warrand nor calling for him bot that lauchfull 
authoritie and ordinar calling quhilk he resavit first of the kirk of God, 
holie and catholik. Gif than his calling was lauchfull and ordinar, the power 
that admittit him was lauchfull and ordinar. Quhat warrand than had he 
to betray the lauchfull power, I mene the kirk of God, catholik, and the 
holie religioun of Jesus Chryste, quhilk they profes. I ame assurit he had 
na uthir warrand nor that quhilk Judas had that betrayit the Lord Jesus 
for xxx d, for as avarice movit Judas, even sa avarice, with the plesour 
of fleschelie lust movit him, for, being refusit of promotioun to ane

LlBRO PRKDICTO, Ca. XV. 

Tulit autem de preda populus [etc. I. Samuel, xv. 21-23]. 

Here wee see that Saul, eftir the grit victore quhilk God gaif him of 
the Amalakitis, saifis frome the ege of the sword Agag thair king, with 
ane grit numbir of the chosin and best of nolt and scheip, and being 
accusit thairof be Samuell, Saul excuses his doing, sayis to the propheit 
that the same was reservit to mak sacrifice to the Lord thairof, nevirtheles 
his excuse is rejectit and his doing is condempnit, albeit the same procedit 
of him to do honour and sacrifice to God ; and that becaus he did nocht 
obey the command of God in puteing man and beist to the ege of the 
sword ; and thairfoir it is said to him : Becaus thou hes nocht hard the 
command of God, God hes cassin the of, that thow sail nocht be king 
ovir Israeli. Now to compair the disobedience of Saul to the rebellioun 
and disobedience of this our age and tyme, quhilk baith prince and pepill 
dois to the kirk of God, quhilk proceidis of that same caus and motioun 
that the rebellioun of Saul and his pepill procedit, quhilk was the inordinat '• wo. 
desyre of the bestiall and spulze of the battell that was grit, even sa the 
inordinat desyre quhilk the lordis, erlis, and baronis and commown pepill 
of this our cuntrie (and of wther cuntreis quhair the kirk of God and hir 
obedience is rejectit) had and yit hes to the kirk rentes, and speciallie the 
teyndis that was promist to the commoun pepill be the preicheris of the 
inconsiderat and feirfull rebellioun, movit thame to reject thee sweit yok 
and obedience of Goddis spoils, his holy catholik kirk, without respect of 
religioun ; and being accusit and demandit of the caus of thair rebellioun, 
they pretend the verie excuse of Saul, to wit, mair holie and sinceir 
wirschip and service of God be reformatioun of relegioun, as they call it, 
bot as the excuse of Saul avalit nocht, na mair sail that thair excuse 
availl thame, albeit the long suffring of God delay his justice, bot as wolfis 
cled in lambskynnis, foirspokin be the Lord Jesus, they salle be cassin away

[Sonnet— Addressed to the Deity. 1 ] 

Supreme essence, begynnyng unbegwn, 
Ay trinall ane, ane undevydit thre, 

Eternall word that victorie hes wun 

Our deth and hell triumphand on the tre, 
Foirknawledge, wisdome, and all seing ee, 

Jehovah, Alpha and Omega, all, 

Like unto non, nor non lyke unto the, 

Unmov't quhilk mov*s the rounds about the ball, 

Contenar unconten't, is, was, and sal 
Be sempiternall, mercifull, and just, 

Creatar uncreatit, now I call, 

Teach me thy trewth since unto the I trust; 

Incres, continue, and kendill frome abufe 

My faith, my hope, bot by the laif my lufe.

of the street leading from the metropolitan church to the market cross, 
between the lands of John Clerk, tailor, citizen, on the east, the lands of 
q. Archibald Herbertsoun on the west, the lands of George Herbertsoun on 
the south, and the lands of Sir Bartholomew Symsoun on the north. Sasine 
given by William Conyghame, bailie. Ratified by Margaret Cochrane. 
Witnesses: George Elphinstone, Mr. Adam Walles, citizens, Peter Andersoun, 
Walter Woddrop in Dalmarnok, George Lowke in Dalbeth, Robert Lettrik 
and John Watsone, officers.

i Ah stated in the footnote to No. 1941, the roause of 
the archdeacon came into the possession of Sir Mathew 
8tcwart of Minto, and subsequently the town house of 
the Duke of Montrose occupied the site. The above 
protocol (No. 2324) narrates how the property parted 
from the archdeaconry. At the time of the Reformation, 
Alexander Dick was archdeacon, and Andrew Beton was 
appointed as his successor in 1560 (see protocol 139H and 
relative footnotes). The church of Peebles had from the 
13th century continued to be the benefice of the arch- 
deacon, and when, subsequent to the Reformation, 
other ecclesiastical dues ceased, the teinds pertaining 
to that church and the Glasgow manse would form the 
main source of revenue. For rebelli >n against the King's 
authority and taking part in the siege of Glasgow castle, 
In May, 1570, Betoun's benefice was forfeited to the 
Crown (Privy Council, Reg. xiv., pp. 64, 05), though it 
may possibly have been restored. When the church 
assembly, held at Leith, in January, 1571-2, made 
arrangements for the election of an archbishop of 
Glasgow, " Mr. James Grer" was, for the purposes of 
thai election, appointed to supply the place of the 



archdeacon in the chapter (Calderwood's Hist. iii. p. 
190) ; but this may have been either ou account of the 
forfeiture or in consequence of Beton being disqualified 
through failing to make "professioun of the true 
religion." The archdeaconry was apparently vacant 
in 1573, a* King James VI., on 8 June of that year, 
presented Archibald Dowglas, brother of the laird of 
Cavers, to the office (Fasti Eoclesim, i. p. 235) The 
Leith assembly had resolved that " all benefices of cure, 
under prelacies, should be disponed to actual ministers, 
and to no others " (Spottiswoode : s History, ii. p. 171) ; 
and as Douglas had, in accordance with the rules then in 
force, to give personal attention in his parochial charge, 
the Glasgow manse could not be used as a residence, and 
he accordingly disposed of it, as mentioned in the 
protocol. The feu-duty is not specified in the protocol, 
but from the crown charter granted to Sir Matthew 
Stewart of Minto, on 22 March, 1596-7, it is mentioned 
that the amount then payable wa* 10 s., with 3 s. 4d. 
of augmentation (Reg. Mag. Sig. vi. No. 5-44.) 

* Written in one place "Joauni," but James appears 
to have been the correct name.

I The collegiate church of Biggar was founded by 
Malcolm lord Fleming, with the concurrence of the 
archbishop and chapter of Glasgow, on 10 January, 
1645 ; and had endowment* for a provost, eight preben- 
daries, four singing boys, and six poor men. John 
8terensoo, precentor of the metropolitan church of 
Glasgow, was the first provost ; and Hunter enumerates 
various subsequent appointment*. The provost named 
in the text was probably William Fleming, son of John 
Fleming of Garwood, who was presented to the provost- 
snip by Lord Fleming on 1st January, 1573 (Biggar and 
the Boose of Fleming, pp. 179-99X 

>Tbe lands of Boghal and Haddokstane, in the shire 
of Renfrew, were confirmed to Malcolm Flemyng of 
Boghal and Isabella of Houstoun, his spouse, by crown 
charter, dated 16 June, 1451 (Reg. Hag. 8ig. ii. No. 568) ; 



and crown charters were also granted to his successors. 
Boghall and Haddockstane are places which can still be 
identified, being situated at a short distance from each 
other in the parish of Houstoun, Renfrewshire. 

Boghall seems to have been latterly the designation 
of a lordship or barony, embracing several lands. In 
the year 1672 the lands of Mylnetoun and Arrotisholl, 
in the parish of Kilbride, were dissolved from the 
barony and mortified by William earl of Dundonald 
(see the first of the two preceding footnotes), and 
William lord Cochrane, his eldest son, to the university 
of Glasgow, for the maintenance of four students of 
philosophy, and also for the support of three students 
of theology (Mun. Aim. Univers. Glasg. i. p. 415 ; The 
Laing Charters, No. 26&X

to him in feu-farm a piece of land called the Gallow Aiker, lying between 
the common muir of the city on the north, the burn called Camlawchce 
Burne on the south, and the lands of Burrofeild on the west.* Charter 
subscribed by notaries on behalf of Andrew Coitis, and by the provost, 
bailies, and councillors of the city in token of their consent, and the

2412. Wilsoun — Lyndsay, spouses. 22 May, 1582, 5 p.m. 

John Hammiltoun of Brwmehill appeared in the porch (podio) of the 
fore tenement aftermentioned, and acknowledged that James Wilsoun and 
Jonet Lyndsay, spouses, had lawfully redeemed an annualrent of £20

with yard and pertinents, lying t.c, on the south side of the Gallowgait, 



l Andrew Coitis, here designated "marshall," wh 
probably the heritable successor of David Coitis, "inair 
of fee " (see protocol No. 713 and relative footnote). 

- Gallowmuir was at one time possessed by the 
community as common pasturage ; but in the year 
1520 sixteen acres were given as an endowment to 
the collegiate church of St. Mary (Olasg. Chart, ii. 
pp. 109-12), and previous to the date of the above 
protocol the remainder had been disposed of by the 
magistrates and council to individual proprietors for 
payment of feuduties or annualrents. Subsequent 
to the Reformation the church acres were likewise 
conveyed to individual purchasers. (See protocols 
151a, 193-4, 213-5, 743, 877-8, 895, 901, 923X Burrofeild, 
on the other hand, does not seem to have belonged 
to the community in ancient times. As shown by 
entries in the Diocesan Registers the lands were held 
by the Bishops' rentallers, and in course of time 
the rental rights were converted into feu holdings. 
Thus on 2 June, 1513, Elizabeth Elphinstoun and 
Peter Culquhoun, her son, were rentalled in the 
40s. land of Bowrrowfeild (Diocesan Reg. i. p. 48). 
In 1520 Thome Forrat was "rentellat in xl s. land 
off the Borrwfeld, and xl s. land of Nycolleys, quhilk 
was the wedos, Augnes Suistar, and off hyr consent, 
becaus he maryt the wedo" (lb. p. 78). After (he 



latter lands had been held on rental for about 
sixty years, Archbishop Boyd, with consent of his 
chapter, granted a charter to William Forret, eldest 
son of Thomas Foret, whereby the "408. land of 
Borroufeild" and the "40s. land of Nicholhoua" 
were appointed to be held by him for payment of 
an annual feuduty of £4, and the charter was 
confirmed by the crown on 24 March, 1685-6 (Reg. 
Mag. Sig. v. No. 958). Similarly, charters were 
granted to Robert Wilkie, minister of Kilmarnock, 
of a 20s. land of Burrowfeld (lb. No. 057), which 
afterwards came into the possession of John Hay, 
rector of Renfrew. The name "Nicolieys" seems to 
have fallen into disuse, and the combined properties were 
latterly known as the £6 laud of Barrowfield. The 
villages of old and new Calton, formed on feus 
given off by the successive owners, were erected 
into a burgh of barony in 1817, but the burgh was 
annexed to Glasgow in 1846. For the history of 
Barrowfield during its possession by the Walkinshaws, 
Orrs, and subsequent proprietors (including the 
City of Glasgow between 1724 and 1731 X reference 
may be made to Mr. John Buchanan's sketch in 
"Glasgow: Past and Present" (1846), pp. 716-80. 
The original feuduties are now payable to the crown.

Arch. Hboatb. 
Vol. L fol. 66. 



l "Royse noble/' an English gold coin marked 
with the device of a rose. By act of parliament 
of the reign of James III. (1466 c. 12) "the new 
noble of the rose" was to have currency in Scotland 
for 25 s. In the Edinburgh accounts for 1662-3, the 
value of the rose noble is put at £3 8 s. Scots 
(Edin. Rec. ii. p. 274). Erekine, in his Institute 
of the Law of Scotland (B. ii. tit 8, s. 2), refers 
to a gratuitous disposition by a father to his son 
being redeemable on payment of a rose noble. 
Several protocols contain a similar provision, so 



that the practice must have long prevailed in this 
country. (See also Stair, B. it. tit. 10, s. 10). A 
grant so made was in effect a donation revocable 
by the father during his lifetime. To obviate the 
inconvenience which might arise from want of a 
specified coin it was provided by the act 1666, 
c. 10, that in the case of lands being redeemable 
for gold or silver "of certane special! valour and 
price or cunzie," if such money could not be had 
within the realm, it would be sufficient to deliver 
current gold or silver money of equal value.

town council gave the dues of admimion of a burgess 
to "Thomas Craig, techear of the new kirk scule, for 
his panes" (Glasg. Kec. i. p. 43). On the 4th of the 
same month 23s. were paid to "Thomas Craige of 
the new kirk scule for straye to the mending thairof 
and for onputting of the samyn" (lb. p. 457). On 
22 February, 1578, £10 were paid to "Thomas Craig 
for his support in teicheing of the new kirk scole" 
(lb. p. 465). The schoolmaster got the gift of a 
chapiainry, as appears from the following entry in 



the city treasurer's accounts for the year 1583-4: 
"Gevin to Thomas Craig, maister of the Trooegait 
scole, for his chaplain rie, viii li." (76. p. 472). From 
the accounts of the previous year it is ascertained 
that the benefice referred to was the prebend 
Nomine Jesu in the collegiate church of St. Mary 
(lb. p. 469). This prebend was founded by Sir 
Martin Reid, a chaplain in the cathedral, and the 
magistrates and council were patrons. (8ee Lib. OolL. 
Nostre boraine, p. xix. ; also protocol No. 1506).

18m footnote to No. 2453. The "new church" was 
acq ui red by James Flemyng in the year 1570, as narrated 
in protocol No. 1702, and at that time the school 
mentioned in the text was called the Song School. It 
had evidently been used in connection with the col- 
legiate church, for the primary purpose of training its 
cb jo ai s tc ia. A similar school was connected with the 
cathedral (see Glasgow Charters, L pp. 65-tt). With 
the changes introduced at the Reformation, elaborate 
choral set-rices ceased, and music culture was to 
some extent abandoned, while instruction in rudi- 
mentary knowledge became more general. When the 
w Inglis Schole " superseded 8t. Man's "sang schole," 
accommodation for the teaching of music was procured 
In 1577 the city treasurer paid 40s. "for 
i (t>., rent) of ane chalmer to be ane sang school " 
(Glasg. Bee. i. p. 402); and in the following year the like 
sum was paid " for the rnaOl of the chalmer to the sang 
scole"(/6. p.463X 

2 The words in the original are:— "Una alia vetus 
ecclesia jam horreo existente cum horto ejusdeni et 
tribos aeris terre exeuntibus et dicte ecclesie Jam horreo 
existente incumbentibas dim vocate Sanct Tenoi* Kirk" 

The church dedicated to 8L Thenau, the mother of 
St Mango, is of unknown antiquity, and it long 
continued a landmark of the city, giving its name to one 
of the principal thoroughfares. After running a course 
of varying ort ho graphy (see Mr. J. T. T. Brown's paper 
la Regality Club, 2nd series, p. 127), the name has settled 
Into its present form, and the 8L Enoch's Church of 
to-day commemorates the existence, if it does not 
oocopy the precise site, of the mediaeval structure. A 
writ, dated 27 October, 1420. refers to the great street 
leading from the market cross to the chapels of St. 



Thomas the Martyr and of St Tanew (Liber Coll. Nostre 
Domine, etc. , p. 244). This is the earliest notice of the 
chapel found in extant records, but subsequent notices, 
including those in the protocols, are numerous. A 
charter, dated in 1175, refers to the chapel where rest 
the bones of St. Thenew, the mother of St. Kcntigern 
(Reg. Episc. Glasg. No. 478) ; but perha|>s this means 
nothing more than that some portions of what were 
accepted as her relics were deposited in the chapel. 
Among the relics preserved in the cathedral in 1432 
were "two small linen bags, with l>ones of SS. 
Kentigern, Thenew, and other divers saint*" (Dillon's 
Inventor}*, translated in Gordon's Scotichronicon, ii. 
p, 452). At the Reformation such objects of veneration 
were considered idolatrous and specially singled out 
for destruction. Any relics then in the chapel would 
therefore, doubtless, disappear. From the above 
protocol it is ascertained tliat the church itself hod been 
converted into a barn. James Fleming, who acquired 
the collegiate church of St. Mary in the Trongate 
(see protocol 1702), had prolwibly got St. Tenew's chapel 
and grounds under similar circumstances. Though 
applied to secular uses, the building seems to have 
retained its external form for some time. Woodrow, 
in his "Collections for the Life of David Weems" 
(Maitland Club, 1848), p. 5, mentions that in the Kirk 
Session Records, under date 5 May, 151)5, "St. Knuis 
Kirk in the Trongate is spoken of. I take it to be 
what is vulgarly called Saint Tennoehis. " MTre, in his 
History of Glasgow, published in 1736, referring to 
churches and chapels before the Reformation, mentions 
"St, Enoch's church, without the West Port, the 
remains of which is yet to be seen " (18*10 edition, p. C2).

1 James Flemyng acquired St Mary's church, on the 
south aide of the Trongait, in 1570, and transferred it to 
the magistrates and council about the year 1592, as nar- 
rated in protocol No. 1702 and relative footnote; and it 
it probable that the " Belhous " was included in these 
transmissions. On 20 December, 1595, instructions were 
given for the town treasurer of the year 1593 accepting 
a sum of money in full of the " maill of the Belhous," 
one half of the" maiU " or rent being remitted "in respect 
the steiple was tane downe" (Rec. i., 175). Another 
steeple, however, appears to hare been erected, as on 
22 February, 1594-5, the provost, bailies, and town 
council, in recognition of the services rendered by 
Archibald Faulls, merchant, in attending to the "re- 
edifying of the Trone Church," for three years, granted 
to htm two booths forming "parts of their New Kirk 
steeple" (Glasgow Charters, L ; Abstract, p. 37, No. 407). 
These two booths were re-acquired by the town council 
in 1631 (76. p. 75, No. 538), at which time the steeple was 
being heightened, and other alterations made, of which 
particulars will be found in the printed records. Thus 
on 27 January, 1630, it was resolved "as ane commoun 
weill of the burgh, that the stiple of the Trongait Kirk 
•ail be hightit in the most best and commodious forme 
can be derysit be the best craftismen, with all diligence 
possible " (Olasg. Rec i. p. 373). Payments towards the 
work were authorised in December, 1630, and February, 
1631 {lb. ii. pp. 1, 2). Then on 19 March, 1631, there 
occurs the following entry in the records which appears 
to refer to the "Belhous" mentioned in the text, as 
distinguished from the steeple erected in connection 



with the renovated church of 1592 ; though, taken in 
conjunction with other passages in the records, it is 
possible that the structures may have been connected : — 
"The provest, bailleis and counsall hes concludit to tak 
doun to the grand the haill bellhous in the Trongait, 
baith new wark and auld " (76. ii. p. 3). 

M'Ure, in his History of Glasgow (1830 edition, p. 59> 
thus descril>es the steeple as it existed in 1736: — "The 
community . . . built a handsome steeple or spear 
before . . . but not adjoining to the church, in the 
year 1637. At the bottom of the steeple there is a trone 
or a place for weighing of goods. . . . The city have 
also furnished the steeple with two large bells that rings 
at six, eight, and ten of the clock at night, and at six in 
the morning, all the year over, and on the Lord's day, 
and other week days of sermon ; but there is no clock in 
it, because it is situate near the tolbooth clock on the 
one hand, and Hutcheson's clock on the other, being all 
three on one street, which, without doubt, is the fairest 
in the whole city. The steeple of this church is 126 foot 
high." The date of erection given by M'Ure has been 
followed by other historians, and that year may have 
been reached before all the fittings were completed, as 
a bell for the steeple was got in 1636 (Glasg. Rec. ii. p. 
41) ; but the work was evidently commenced some years 
earlier. In consequence of its detached position the 
steeple esca|>ed the conflagration by which the church 
was destroyed in 1793 ; and at the present day it forms 
one of the most interesting relics of Old Glasgow, with 
a history which can be traced back to 1631, if not forty 
years earlier.

i"Aa to aangsters, precentors, and readers, the 
Beoords (£*, of the Kirk SessionX Sept 7, 1587, ordean 
Mr. William Strutters, teacher of musick, shall sing in 
the High Kirk, from the ringing of the first bell to the 
minister's coming in ; and appoint four men to sit beside

1588, March 14, that the sangsters in toun 
with Mr. William Struthers on 8unday" (Wodrow's

Club), pp. 22, 23). In the town's accounts for 1583-4, the 
sum of 40 s. was paid to "Mr. William Strutheris for to 
pay the maiU of ane sange scole" (Qlasg. Rec. i. p. 472). 
The "reader'* and "teacher of musick" are, it is 
presumed, identical with the "Sir William Strutheris" 
who, in 1576-7, was paid 40 s. " for male of ane chalmer 
to be ane sang scole" (lb. p. 462). The prefix "Sir" 
indicated that Struthers was a chaplain, and the " Mr." 
shows that subsequently he had acquired the degree of 
master of arts.

iThe Annualrent is stated to be redeemable for 40 
merks, bat this may be a clerical slip, as the redemption 
mooey was usually the same as the price. A return of 
6 merks on £40 represents about 8 per cent., a not 
unusual rate at that period, though 10 per cent, was 
more common. According to the canon law, enforceable 
in this country previous to the Reformation, a stipula- 
tion for payment of interest was uot allowed ; but after 
that law fell into abeyance, there was for some time no 



limit to the rate which might be stipulated. A Scottish 
statute G&87, c. 52 of the 1681 edition of the acts ; 1587, 
c. S5, supplemented by 1597, c. 18 of the Record edition) 
restricted the profit to be taken for the loan of money to 
10 per cent; and the legal rate was from time to time 
reduced by subsequent acts of parliament (see Erskine's 
Institute, B. iii. tit. iii. s. 75, 76). These limitations, 
however, were abolished by the "Usury Laws Repeal 
Act" of 1854.

I. — Abstracts of Henry Oibsone's Protocols, 1584-3. 1 

2598. Hammyltonk— Barnclwch. 10 November, 1584, 3 p.m. 

John Herbertsone, son of q. Mr. Robert Herbertsone, sold to Mr. John 
Hammiltoun of Barnclwythtow, the lands called Deneside Croft^ then consisting 
of yards (nunc existcntibus in hortis), with houses, woods and pertinents, 
lying in the parish of Glasgow, near the city thereof, between the lands 
of and q. Robert Baxter on the east, the common vennel on 

the west, the street of Ratounraw on the north, and another street on the 
south. 1 Sasine given by John Robertoun, bailie of the lord subdean of 
Glasgow and of the lands of the subdeanery. Witnesses : Mr. Patrick 
Scharpe, regent in the college of Glasgow, William Sutherland, Archibald 
Lettrik, and Thomas Diksoun, officer. 

2599. Craufurd—Jordanhill— Ker, 17 November, 1584, 1 p.m. 
Thomas Craufurd of Jordanhill and Jonet Ker, spouses, produced a 



i No*. 2606 to 2001 inclusive are abstract* of protocols 
contained in a leaf which had belonged to one of Henry 
Oibsone's misting volumes (see footnote to No. 2314). 
The date 1684 had apparently been misread 1574, and 
the leaf was placed in Oibsone's second MS. volume, 
among; the protocols of the latter year. In the volume 
as now re-bound it has been placed at the end. 

sin 14*4, Archibald Whitelaw, subdean of Glasgow, 
endowed the chaplainry of St John the Baptist with 
two acres of land in the Deanside ; and his successor, 
Roland Blacader, made a similar donation (see Arch- 
bishop Eyre's paper on "The Old Arrangements of 
the Glasgow Cathedral" in Transactions of the Glasgow 
ArcbjBological Society, new series, i. 483 ; and authorities 
cited ; also Bladder's deed of foundation, printed in 
Glasgow Protocols, ii. No. 618). As stated in protocol 
No. 1376, SirWilliam Crawfurd, chaplain of the chaplainry 
of St John, feued six acres of land in the " Oeynsyd" 
to Mr. Robert Berbertsoun, whose son disposed of the 
property as set forth in the above protocol No. 2598. 

The lands contained in the protocol appear to be part 
of those acquired by Hutchesons' Hospital from Andrew 



Cochrane In 1741, and transferred by the hospital to 
the magistrates and council of Glasgow in 1772 (MS. 
Inventory of City Writs, ii. b. 25). In the disposition to 
the hospital In 1741 the property is described as " these 
seven acres and a half of land or thereby, now inclosed 
in a yeard, lying within the territory of the burgh of 
Glasgow, in that croft called the Deanside; bounded 
between the lands of (blank) on the west, Ramshom 
yeard on the south, the lands of (blank) on the east, 
and the Ratten raw Street on the north ; together with 
that large barn, thereafter a dwellinghouse and barn, 
the dwellinghouse being now ruinous, adjoining to and 
on the north side of the said yeard ; as the same are 
presently possest by John Maitland, John Garner, James 
Maitland, William Aikine, Arthur Hamilton, Duncan 
M'Auslan, William Reid, and John Logan, all gardinerts 
in Glasgow." Deanside was disposed of by the magis- 
trates and council, in building lots, about the year 1787, 
and is now occupied by buildings, b e t wee n George 
Street and Rottenrow, a little to the east of Frederick 
Street.

2602. Mwdy. 29 July, 1577, 8 a.m. 

John Wilsoun, bailie, gave sasine to John Mwdy as heir of q. James 
Mwdy, maltman, citizen, his father, of a tenement, fore and back, with 
yard and pertinents, lying t.c, on the west side of the street called 
Stokudgate, between the lands of James Syare on the north, the lands of 
Robert Wilsoun on the west, the Common Green (commune viridarium) of 
Glasgow on the south, and the street on the east. Thereafter, John Mwdy, 
for implement of a contract of marriage between him on the one part, and 
John Gibsone in Govane and Jonet Gibsone, his daughter, on the other part, 
resigned in favour of himself and Jonet Gibsone, and 1.1., (1) the property 
above mentioned; and (2) a kiln, with yard adjacent thereto, and the 
pertinents, lying t.c, on the south side of the street of St. Tenu, between 
the lands of David Spreull and the yard of the laird of Fulbar on the 
east, the lands of Thomas Scot and John Johnsoun on the west, and the 
lands of Walter Landes on the south. Witnesses: William Hill, Gilbert 
Newlandis, Patrick Maxuell, Archibald Sellare, citizens, and Robert Lettrik, 
officer. 

2603. Morsone. 22 August, 1578, 9 a.m. 

Eduard Pollok, citizen, and Beatrix Morsone, spouses, sold to Felane 
Morsone, son of Andrew Morsone, cooper (trietoris), citizen, the equal half of 
a back tenement, with close, yard and pertinents, and free ingress and 
egress by the fore gate and close, pro indiviso, lying t.c, on the north 
side of the Briggate, between the lands of q. Patrick Patersone and Thomas 
Bargille on the south, the lands of George Herbertsone on the east, the 
lands of q. Richard Ros and Patrick Patersone on the west, and the lands 
of q. Michael Lyndsaye on the north. Sasine given by Robert Rowat, 
bailie. Witnesses : James Law, George Young, John Herbertsone, Felane 
Snype, Thomas Mylne, citizens, and John Paw tone, officer.

III.— Abstract of Instrument of Sasine by William Hegait, notary, 

IN FAVOUR OF RlCHARD KlRKLAND. 1 

2604. Rirkland, 10 April, 1567, 11 a.m. 

George Kirkland, burgess of the burgh and city of Glasgow, for the 
love and affection he bore towards Richard Kirkland, his first born sons 
resigned in his favour the west half of a tenement and land, with yard, 
and pertinents, lying in the street commonly called Drygait (via arida\ 
between the lands of John Ouchter on the west, of William Biggart on 
the east, the burn of Malindonor on the north, and the common street on the 
south; but reserving the liferent of George Kirkland.' Sasine given by Mr. 
Adam Wallace. Witnesses: James Flemyng, burgess, Duncan Fynlay, John 
Allasone, tailor, Gilbert Hail, and John Fowler, Serjeant. 

"Et Ego vero Willelmus Hegait, clericus 
Glasguensis diocesis, auctoritate apos- 
tolica perprius, notarius publicus, ac 
nunc per dominos consilij, secundum 
tenorem acti parliamenti admissus, quia 
premissis omnibus et singulis dum ut 
premittitur dicerentur agerentur et 
fierent, una cum prenominatis testibus 
presens personaliter interfui, eaque 
singula sic fieri vidi scivi et audivi; 
ac in notam cepi ex qua hoc presens 
publicum instrumentum manu aliena me 
aliis occupato exinde confeci et signavi, 
in fidem et testimonium veritatis 
omnium premissorum rogatus et re- 
quisites."
